OIMDB — THE DESIGNS WE REJECTED, MEASURED BESIDE THE ONE WE SHIPPED

Each row is one architectural decision. `ours` is the design that shipped; `floor` is the design
that was proposed and refused. Not a primitive — a candidate. The ratio is still ours over the
floor, and it is a verdict on a decision rather than a price tag.

node v24.16.0   8 cores   foreign-load gate 99.00   gc exposed

THE INSTRUMENT, BEFORE ANY VERDICT

ratio   ours  floor  noise  expected  control
1.94x  37 ns  19 ns   0.3%     ~1.9x  control-resolution-2x
1.01x  19 ns  19 ns   0.1%     1.00x  control-scale-same-body

  The resolution control saw 1.94x for a deliberate doubling of work, so this instrument resolves a 2x.
  The scale control — the same body under two names — came out 0.7% from 1.00.
  THE RULE FOR THE WHOLE TABLE: no ratio within 0.7% of 1.00 is a reading.

  AND IT BITES HARDER HERE THAN ON A PRICE TABLE. A price of 34x survives a 6% noise floor without
  a scratch — nobody rereads a 34x because the instrument wobbles by a twentieth of it. A decision
  that wins by 4% does not survive it at all: the margin IS the instrument. Such a row is not a
  narrow win and it is not a tie either — the decision is UNSETTLED, which is a different sentence
  from "the two designs are equal". Equal is a claim about the designs; unsettled is a statement
  about this machine at this moment, and a quieter one may separate them. Those rows print no
  margin, because a margin the instrument could not resolve is what gets quoted on.

HOW TO READ A ROW HERE — THE OPPOSITE OF THE PRICE TABLE

  ratio = ours / floor, where the floor is THE DESIGN WE REJECTED. So the direction is inverted
  against every other table in this suite:

    below 1.00   the shipped design wins. The margin column says by how much: it is 1 / ratio,
                 because 0.40x is the ratio and "2.5x cheaper" is what a person means by it.
    above 1.00   THE REJECTED DESIGN WON AND THE DECISION WAS WRONG. Marked `!`, and it is a
                 finding rather than an error in the harness. Several pairs in this catalogue were
                 written by their authors EXPECTING to lose — a delivery list against a scan at
                 50% dirty, coalescing at one write per node per frame, a field filter at one in
                 four skipped — because a comparison that only ever vindicates its author is a
                 comparison nobody should believe. Those rows are the most valuable in the suite.

  !  THE REJECTED DESIGN WON THIS ROW. A finding, not a failure of the harness —
     read the caveat under it before deciding what it settles. A row carrying both
     `!` and `=` lost by less than the instrument can resolve: unsettled, not lost.
  =  the claim is no larger than the instrument's own disagreement. Not a reading,
     and on this table that means the decision is unsettled rather than the two
     designs being equal.
  ~  the spread inside the process exceeded 30%: the machine was busy, not the code.
  L  foreign load rose above the gate while this pair was measured.
  w  a side reached the warm-up ceiling before the planned count. It entered the
     clock at a lower V8 tier than its partner; the counts are under each row.

  59 of 193 decisions stand on this run, 111 came out wrong, 23 are unsettled.

WHERE THE TWO SIDES ARE NOT DOING THE SAME WORK

On a price table an unequal floor flatters us and that is the whole risk: a primitive doing less
than our call makes the abstraction look cheaper than it is. HERE THE RISK IS A DIFFERENT AND WORSE
ONE. A floor that is not the rejected design faithfully built means we may have refused a design we
never actually measured — and then printed a number saying we were right to. That is the failure this
catalogue exists to prevent, and it cannot be checked from a ratio.

So it is checked from the caveats, which are printed in full under every row below. Each one names
what the rejected design would have BOUGHT — the reason somebody proposed it — before it names
anything else, and states which way each inequality pushes that row's ratio. No claim is summarised
here that a row does not make for itself.

ONE THING IS STRUCTURAL AND APPLIES WHEREVER BOTH ARMS RUN THE ENGINE: the price tables put our code
against a `Map` that nothing of ours can spoil, and that is no longer true when both sides call the
same compiled function through the same inline caches. One side's shapes then reach the other's
measurement inside one process. The rows where that applies say so, and say which way it leans.

alt.dirty-list-vs-scan.1-of-1024-marked   [per frame]   0.04x
  verdict  the shipped design wins — 25.7x cheaper
  ours     a delivery list built at the write — the frame walks only what was marked
  floor    a stamp written at the write — the frame scans every subscriber for this frame's stamp
  ONE OPERATION IS ONE FRAME: the stated number of subscribers out of the population are
  marked, spread evenly, and the frame delivers to exactly those. The list pays a flag test
  and a push per mark and walks only the marked; the scan pays one store per mark and walks
  the whole population every frame. So the answer turns on the marked fraction and THE ROWS
  CROSS OVER: read them as a table, never as one ratio. Both arms were checked to deliver to
  the same number of subscribers before the clock.
  measured  1,336,652 ops/pass ours, 49,550 floor; 9 samples; warm-up 20 planned, 20/20 reached; spread 0.7%/13.8%; load 3.01 → 3.01 on 8 cores

alt.dirty-flag-vs-set.65536-carriers.4-marks-each   [per write]   0.07x
  verdict  the shipped design wins — 13.5x cheaper
  ours     if (!carrier.isPending) { carrier.isPending = true; pending.push(carrier) } — drained as an array
  floor    pendingSet.add(carrier) — drained by iterating the Set and clearing it
  ONE OPERATION IS ONE MARK. A frame marks every carrier the stated number of times, in passes
  over the whole population, and then drains, so a repeat mark is the flag's early return on
  ours and a hash hit on the Set. The drain runs one counting body per carrier on both arms
  and both were checked to run the same number before the clock. This is the shape of
  `OIMChannel.addEffectScheduledToRun` (the `isRunScheduled` flag and a push) and its frame
  loop, hand-written, so one carrier shape reaches each arm; production effects are built by
  one object literal in OIMEffectService, so the engine also sees one shape there.
  measured  17,915,282 ops/pass ours, 804,512 floor; 9 samples; warm-up 27 planned, 27/27 reached; spread 6.3%/8.2%; load 3.19 → 3.19 on 8 cores

alt.prepend-list-vs-positions-map   [per write]   0.09x
  verdict  the shipped design wins — 11.2x cheaper
  ours     bucketCollection.bucketContentService.insertBucketMemberSlotAt(bucket, 0, slot) into a list of 1024, membership kept in a Set
  floor    the same prepend, membership kept in a Map to positions
  THE OPERATION COUNT IS NOT IN DISPUTE. A prepend splices one member in at the front, which
  invalidates every position after it: the renumber is exactly `list.length - at` map writes,
  so 1025 of them here, against one memmove and one `Set.add` on our side. EACH OPERATION IS A
  PREPEND AND A REMOVAL OF THE TAIL, so the list stays the length it is named for; the removal
  renumbers nothing on either design. Ours is `OIMBucketSlotsServiceArrayDriven` over
  `OIMBucketArrayMembershipUnique`; the floor is the same service subclassed to keep a
  positions map. DIRECTION: the bucket collection's own epilogue — the hold, the unhold, the
  `channel.writeWithoutFlushCheck` and the emptiness update — is identical on both halves and
  inside both numbers, which pulls this toward 1.00. A LAZY positions map is a third design
  and this row does not refute it. The reading in
  `packages/bench/measurements/2026-08-20-alternatives/positions` was taken on a positional
  index and bucket mutator that are gone and is not re-attributed here.
  measured  41,958 ops/pass ours, 2,346 floor; 9 samples; warm-up 30 planned, 30/30 reached; spread 0.8%/1.9%; load 2.00 → 2.08 on 8 cores

alt.memo.node-vs-recompute.dear-body.16-reads-per-write   [per read]   0.11x
  verdict  the shipped design wins — 8.78x cheaper
  ours     getValueForSlot — a held computed slot of an OIMComputedCollection over the list slot, derived once per write
  floor    body(list.rows) — the same body, called on every read, no node
  THE CASE A MEMO IS FOR: the dear body, sixteen reads between two writes — or sixteen
  consumers reading once each, which is the same axis. Ours derives once and answers fifteen
  reads from the node; the floor walks the list sixteen times. BOTH ARMS WRITE THE SAME LIST
  THROUGH `channel.write` before each run of reads, so the stamp is charged equally and only
  the reads differ. Ours asks the node, which compares its clock, walks its one source's stamp
  and runs the body once after each write; the floor calls the body on every read. Nothing is
  subscribed, so no frame runs on either side. The list does not change between writes, so the
  node's guard finds the value equal and keeps it — the cheapest outcome for ours after a
  derive. The node is a computed slot resolved before the clock, and every read goes through
  its owner's `getValueForSlot`, which pays `assertNotReleased` before the clock compare; its
  derive finds the list by the slot's key, one array index the floor's direct `list.rows` does
  not pay.
  measured  2,097,152 ops/pass ours, 327,752 floor; 9 samples; warm-up 23 planned, 23/23 reached; spread 0.7%/7.9%; load 1.91 → 1.91 on 8 cores

alt.carrier-emitter-vs-key-handler-map.mark   [per row per write]   0.15x
  verdict  the shipped design wins — 6.69x cheaper
  ours     mark a carrier: read its own effects list, compare its stamp, enlist, stamp it
  floor    mark a key: probe a Map<key, handlers>, dedup the key in a Set, enlist
  THE MARK, NOT THE DELIVERY. Ours transcribes the prefix of
  `OIMChannel.writeWithoutFlushCheck` — `slot.effects`, the stamp against the frame, the
  `isRunScheduled` check of `addEffectScheduledToRun` and the stamp store — without the clock
  wrap and the flush request; the floor is the self-contained emitter the post turned down,
  one hash to find the handlers and one string-`Set` insertion to dedup the key. Every key
  carries one standing effect and a frame marks 1024 distinct keys once each, so every mark
  enlists on both arms; closing the frame is inside both numbers once per 1024 marks. The
  build throws unless one frame enlists the same count on both. What the floor buys and ours
  does not: carriers know nothing about subscriptions, and a key with no carrier yet can be
  subscribed to. THE POST'S ENGINE IS NOT THIS ENGINE. All four posts describe `@oimdb/core`
  3.x, which now lives in `packages/core-legacy/`; this row runs hand-written arms shaped
  after the current engine, so it settles the DECISION and settles NOTHING about the withdrawn
  table.
  measured  6,458,506 ops/pass ours, 666,560 floor; 9 samples; warm-up 23 planned, 23/23 reached; spread 1.0%/2.4%; load 2.39 → 2.39 on 8 cores

alt.frame-dedup.stamp-vs-set.fan-in-3   [per row per write]   0.21x
  verdict  the shipped design wins — 4.67x cheaper
  ours     the same three lines, at fan-in 3 — two arrivals in three are repeats
  floor    the same Set, at fan-in 3 — two arrivals in three are repeats
  THE GEOMETRY THE QUOTED FIGURES WERE TAKEN AT — 200 consumers, fan-in 3, 300 roots — and the
  row that answers whether 0.95 ns against 5.24 ns, a factor of 5.5x, is what this mechanism
  costs [NO RUN BEHIND THIS FIGURE]. ONE THING ABOUT THE MIX IS NOT THE SAME AND IS SAID
  RATHER THAN LEFT TO BE FOUND: the quoted run reported 370 arrivals per frame of which 182
  were deduplicated, and this row writes EVERY root, so a frame here is 600 arrivals of which
  400 are repeats [NO RUN BEHIND THIS FIGURE]. The unit is per arrival on both, which is what
  makes them comparable at all; the repeat FRACTION is higher here, and since a repeat is
  where the stamp is strongest, this row should read at least as favourably as the quoted one
  and a reading below it is the interesting outcome [FIGURE STATED IN ADVANCE]. RESIDUALS.
  WHAT THE `Set` BUYS, and it is not nothing: dedup and containment are ONE operation, there
  is no per-leaf word to keep in step, and the invariant the stamp design holds by hand — a
  leaf is on the frame's list iff its stamp says so — cannot be broken, so that arm cannot
  list a leaf twice or lose one. The stamp buys back two things the `Set` cannot: it is a
  FRAME NUMBER, so it is stale again at the next frame with nobody clearing it, and it is also
  the DETACH — a leaf let go of between the write that listed it and the flush is unlisted by
  writing the same field, and the frame's pass reads it again before calling. A `Set` design
  needs a second mechanism for that and this arm is not charged for one. WHAT THE ENGINE DOES
  TODAY IS NEITHER ARM AS PRICED: `TOIMEffect.isRunScheduled` is a BOOLEAN, which the frame's
  pass clears as it consumes a leaf and `detach` writes false — the same compare and store, so
  the operation count here stands, and the self-staling property this arm is credited with is
  one the boolean does not have. The row is a reading of the frame-number arm and owes a run
  against the boolean [NO RUN BEHIND THIS FIGURE]. AGAINST US: our arm carries a frame number
  and pays a compare and a store the `Set` arm does not, because the stamp is doing the
  queue's dedup as well as its own. AGAINST THE `Set` ARM: its `clear()` is proportional to
  what it held where our `length = 0` is not, and both are inside the operation count here
  rather than hidden outside it. WHAT NEITHER ARM PAYS: the write that produced the arrival
  and the walk of `slot.effects` that carried it, which are `OIMChannel.write`'s and identical
  on both sides.
  measured  14,947,986 ops/pass ours, 2,097,152 floor; 9 samples; warm-up 24 planned, 24/24 reached; spread 1.3%/17.7%; load 2.07 → 2.15 on 8 cores

alt.frame-dedup.stamp-vs-set.fan-in-1   [per row per write]   0.23x
  verdict  the shipped design wins — 4.39x cheaper
  ours     compare the leaf's frame stamp, write it, push onto the frame array — no arrival is a repeat
  floor    add the leaf to the frame's Set, which dedups by containment — no arrival is a repeat
  THE ROW WITH NOTHING TO DEDUPLICATE, AND IT IS HERE BECAUSE IT IS THE ONE THE STAMP SHOULD
  WIN LEAST. Every leaf reads one root, so all 200 arrivals of a frame are first arrivals: our
  stamp never takes its early return and the `Set` never probes an entry it already holds.
  What is left in the difference is a field compare plus a field store plus an array push
  against one hash insert, with no repeat path exercised on either side. A reader who wants to
  know what the stamp is worth when the graph is a tree — one consumer per root, which is what
  most screens are — reads this row and not the other two. It is also the row that says
  whether the recovered figures can be quoted at all: if the stamp does not lead here, the
  mechanism is not what the quoted per-arrival numbers were describing. RESIDUALS. WHAT THE
  `Set` BUYS, and it is not nothing: dedup and containment are ONE operation, there is no
  per-leaf word to keep in step, and the invariant the stamp design holds by hand — a leaf is
  on the frame's list iff its stamp says so — cannot be broken, so that arm cannot list a leaf
  twice or lose one. The stamp buys back two things the `Set` cannot: it is a FRAME NUMBER, so
  it is stale again at the next frame with nobody clearing it, and it is also the DETACH — a
  leaf let go of between the write that listed it and the flush is unlisted by writing the
  same field, and the frame's pass reads it again before calling. A `Set` design needs a
  second mechanism for that and this arm is not charged for one. WHAT THE ENGINE DOES TODAY IS
  NEITHER ARM AS PRICED: `TOIMEffect.isRunScheduled` is a BOOLEAN, which the frame's pass
  clears as it consumes a leaf and `detach` writes false — the same compare and store, so the
  operation count here stands, and the self-staling property this arm is credited with is one
  the boolean does not have. The row is a reading of the frame-number arm and owes a run
  against the boolean [NO RUN BEHIND THIS FIGURE]. AGAINST US: our arm carries a frame number
  and pays a compare and a store the `Set` arm does not, because the stamp is doing the
  queue's dedup as well as its own. AGAINST THE `Set` ARM: its `clear()` is proportional to
  what it held where our `length = 0` is not, and both are inside the operation count here
  rather than hidden outside it. WHAT NEITHER ARM PAYS: the write that produced the arrival
  and the walk of `slot.effects` that carried it, which are `OIMChannel.write`'s and identical
  on both sides.
  measured  7,172,940 ops/pass ours, 1,045,398 floor; 9 samples; warm-up 28 planned, 28/28 reached; spread 2.0%/3.6%; load 2.17 → 2.17 on 8 cores

alt.frame-dedup.stamp-vs-set.fan-in-8   [per row per write]   0.30x
  verdict  the shipped design wins — 3.30x cheaper
  ours     the same three lines, at fan-in 8 — seven arrivals in eight are repeats
  floor    the same Set, at fan-in 8 — seven arrivals in eight are repeats
  THE WIDE END, WHERE ALMOST EVERY ARRIVAL IS A REPEAT AND THE TWO DESIGNS ARE FURTHEST APART
  PER ARRIVAL. A leaf reading eight roots is an ordinary derived view — a total over a group,
  a row joined from several records — and a frame that touches all of them offers it eight
  times. Our arm answers seven of those eight with one field compare and a branch; the `Set`
  answers each with a hash of the object identity and a bucket probe. THIS IS THE ROW TO
  DISTRUST IF ANY, and in the direction that flatters us: the frame's own walk and its
  teardown are shared by both arms and are amortised over 1600 arrivals here against 200 at
  fan-in 1, so the shared constant is at its smallest exactly where our margin is at its
  largest. Read the three rows as a table and the trend across them rather than any one of
  them as the price. RESIDUALS. WHAT THE `Set` BUYS, and it is not nothing: dedup and
  containment are ONE operation, there is no per-leaf word to keep in step, and the invariant
  the stamp design holds by hand — a leaf is on the frame's list iff its stamp says so —
  cannot be broken, so that arm cannot list a leaf twice or lose one. The stamp buys back two
  things the `Set` cannot: it is a FRAME NUMBER, so it is stale again at the next frame with
  nobody clearing it, and it is also the DETACH — a leaf let go of between the write that
  listed it and the flush is unlisted by writing the same field, and the frame's pass reads it
  again before calling. A `Set` design needs a second mechanism for that and this arm is not
  charged for one. WHAT THE ENGINE DOES TODAY IS NEITHER ARM AS PRICED:
  `TOIMEffect.isRunScheduled` is a BOOLEAN, which the frame's pass clears as it consumes a
  leaf and `detach` writes false — the same compare and store, so the operation count here
  stands, and the self-staling property this arm is credited with is one the boolean does not
  have. The row is a reading of the frame-number arm and owes a run against the boolean [NO
  RUN BEHIND THIS FIGURE]. AGAINST US: our arm carries a frame number and pays a compare and a
  store the `Set` arm does not, because the stamp is doing the queue's dedup as well as its
  own. AGAINST THE `Set` ARM: its `clear()` is proportional to what it held where our `length
  = 0` is not, and both are inside the operation count here rather than hidden outside it.
  WHAT NEITHER ARM PAYS: the write that produced the arrival and the walk of `slot.effects`
  that carried it, which are `OIMChannel.write`'s and identical on both sides.
  measured  9,077,138 ops/pass ours, 2,363,441 floor; 9 samples; warm-up 35 planned, 35/35 reached; spread 1.7%/2.7%; load 2.15 → 2.28 on 8 cores

alt.identity-store.one-map-vs-two.resolve-a-key-to-an-identity   [per relationship]   0.30x
  verdict  the shipped design wins — 3.28x cheaper
  ours     map.get(key) — the identity already exists, the row is on it
  floor    identities.get(key) ?? mint and file one — the first subscription to that key
  A SUBSCRIPTION TURNING A KEY INTO AN IDENTITY, the first time each key is asked. Ours minted
  every identity at the load, so a resolve is one hash; the two-map store mints on demand, so
  the first resolve of a key is a miss, a mint and a second `Map.set`. The floor's identity
  map is emptied between passes, untimed, so every pass is that first resolve; a key resolved
  twice would cost the floor one hash, as ours. The mint ours paid at the load is priced by
  the write row, not here.

alt.enqueue-by-reference-vs-disposer-closure.array-queue   [per row per write]   0.34x
  verdict  the shipped design wins — 2.91x cheaper
  ours     enqueue the effect itself, deduplicated by its own isPending flag
  floor    enqueue a record and hand back a disposer closure that cancels it
  OURS IS THE SHAPE `OIMChannel` SHIPS — the private `addEffectScheduledToRun` pushes the
  effect by reference and dedups on `effect.isRunScheduled` — transcribed rather than called,
  because the public `scheduleEffectRun` also requests a flush. The floor allocates a record
  and a closure per enqueue, which is what the post turned down; its disposers are sunk so
  neither allocation can be removed. A frame enqueues 1024 distinct effects once each and then
  drains, and the drain is inside both numbers once per frame. The build throws unless one
  drain runs the same count on both. THE POST'S ENGINE IS NOT THIS ENGINE. All four posts
  describe `@oimdb/core` 3.x, which now lives in `packages/core-legacy/`; this row runs
  hand-written arms shaped after the current engine, so it settles the DECISION and settles
  NOTHING about the withdrawn table.

alt.coalesce-vs-eager.16-writes-per-node-per-frame   [per frame]   0.35x
  verdict  the shipped design wins — 2.88x cheaper
  ours     mark the node at the write, recompute each marked node once when the frame closes
  floor    recompute the node at every write
  ONE OPERATION IS ONE FRAME over the whole node population, each node taking the stated
  number of writes to its sources. Coalescing pays a flag test and a push per write and one
  recompute per node per frame; eager pays one recompute per write and nothing else. So the
  answer is a BREAK-EVEN in writes per node per frame, not a speed-up: at one write eager does
  strictly less, and every row above it is where coalescing has to earn its list. Both arms
  are plain objects; both were checked to leave every node holding the same value after a
  frame.

alt.computed-dedup.getter-vs-channel.few-read-once   [per write]   0.38x
  verdict  the shipped design wins — 2.61x cheaper
  ours     a frame of 16 writes; 16 of 256 derivations read once each — deduped at the getter
  floor    the same frame — deduped in the channel, by a dependent walk and a frame stamp
  THE SHAPE AN APPLICATION USUALLY HAS, and the one the shipped arm is built for: far more
  derivations exist than any one frame reads. Ours settles 16 derivations and touches nothing
  else; the channel arm walks every dependent of all 16 written roots and recomputes each
  queued derivation whether or not it is read. ABOVE 1.0x HERE WOULD BE THE FINDING [FIGURE
  STATED IN ADVANCE], because it would mean the per-read source comparison costs more than
  recomputing derivations nobody wants. Both arms write through the same `OIMEntityCollection`
  and `OIMChannel`, so the write path is common and cancels; what differs is only where the
  "already done this frame" question is asked. What no ratio here prices: the getter arm is
  what makes a derivation nobody reads cost nothing at all, which is a property of the whole
  engine and not of this frame.

alt.replacing-build.decide-first-vs-build-as-you-walk.unchanged-resend   [per write]   0.39x
  verdict  the shipped design wins — 2.57x cheaper
  ours     the replacing writer: walk, find nothing moved, allocate nothing
  floor    REJECTED: copy the row up front, assign what moved, store the copy regardless
  THE WRITE THE SHIPPED ORDER EXISTS FOR. "Decide before you allocate" only differs from
  "build as you walk" on a write that moves NOTHING, and there it differs completely: ours
  returns false having allocated neither a row nor a names array, and the floor has already
  spread the row before it could know. ONE OPERATION IS ONE UNCHANGED `updateEntity` OF A
  SIXTEEN-COLUMN ROW, primed outside the clock. Both arms: 16 `oimIsProtoKey` calls, 16
  equality calls, one `oimIsPlain`, no mark. The floor allocates one seventeen-property
  record, copies seventeen values into it and stores it on the slot — per write, abandoned.
  THE COPY IS STORED, so it escapes and V8 cannot elide it; storing it is also the faithful
  reading of the rejected design, which has no decision point at which to withhold the copy —
  and so hands readers a new reference on an unchanged write. THIS ROW MEASURES WHAT THAT
  COSTS AND NOT WHETHER IT IS ACCEPTABLE. Read with the `every-column-moved` row beside it.
  THE WRITE AROUND THE WALK IS IDENTICAL ON BOTH ARMS AND IS INSIDE BOTH NUMBERS:
  `assertWritable` with the channel's `assertNotFlushing`, `openSubframe`/`closeSubframe`, the
  writer dispatch, the `length > 0` test, and — on a write that moves something — the
  changed-names array, `markColumnsChanged`'s column-store probe per moved column,
  `channel.writeWithoutFlushCheck` and `markContentChanged`. That is a large constant added to
  the numerator and the denominator alike, so it pulls this ratio toward 1.00 and the printed
  ratio is a LOWER BOUND on the difference between the two walks. The two nanosecond columns
  beside it are not pulled anywhere: subtract them to get the per-write difference the row is
  actually about. THIS PAIR DECLARES `isolate`, AND THE READING IS UNINTERPRETABLE WITHOUT IT.
  Both arms reach the engine through one compiled function —
  `OIMEntityCollection.prototype.patchEntity`, whose single call site
  `this.entityWriter.patchEntity(entitySlot, patch)` would see both writers in one process.
  Neither would then be inlined, and inlining the column walk into the write is exactly where
  a walk's advantage lives; worse, the failure is invisible, because a ratio of 1.00 would be
  equally consistent with "the two walks cost the same" and "each arm ran through caches the
  other had already made polymorphic". Each side is therefore probed and timed in a process
  that has never constructed the other, and `build` declines to fill the population it was not
  asked for — merely FILLING the other design's population teaches V8 its shape, so declining
  to time it is not enough. What it costs: `noise` now includes process-to-process variation,
  which is the honest thing for it to include here.
  measured  291,322 ops/pass ours, 65,536 floor; 9 samples; warm-up 27 planned, 27/27 reached; spread 40.2%/4.4%; load 2.63 → 2.63 on 8 cores

alt.replacement-rebuild.survivors-vs-spread-and-delete.read-after   [per read]   0.43x
  verdict  the shipped design wins — 2.32x cheaper
  ours     read a surviving column off a replacement built empty and filled with survivors
  floor    REJECTED: the same read off a replacement spread and then deleted from
  THE SECOND READING, AND THE ONE THE SHIPPED SIDE IS BUYING. `delete` on a plain object takes
  it out of the shape its population shares, and the rejected rebuild would hand readers a
  replacement born in that state on every removal. That cost is paid PER READ, for the life of
  the row. ONE OPERATION IS ONE PROPERTY READ of a SURVIVING column (`c07`) off one stored

  `c15` reads `undefined` on both sides and would measure a miss. EVERY ROW LOST THE COLUMN,
  so each arm sees exactly one shape — the conservative form. THIS PAIR DOES NOT DECLARE
  `isolate`, AND THAT IS A DECISION RATHER THAN A DEFAULT. The timed body on each arm is a
  hand-written read loop belonging to that arm alone — no library code is entered by either,
  so there is no shared compiled function whose inline caches the two arms could spoil for
  each other. Each loop's property access therefore sees exactly the row shape its own design
  produces, for the life of the process, which is the whole thing being measured. The
  populations ARE built through the shipped `upsertEntity` with two different writers, and
  that build is outside the clock; nothing timed here touches it. Keeping the ordinary
  single-process form buys the floor-twice bracket, which is the stronger drift check and is

alt.equality-predicate.row-comparator-vs-column-comparators.one-column-of-sixteen   [per write]   0.67x
  verdict  the shipped design wins — 1.50x cheaper
  ours     a population built with one comparator for the whole row — asked on all sixteen
  floor    REJECTED: a map from column name to predicate, plain === for the other fifteen
  WHAT A CALLER REACHING FOR THE PARAMETER ALMOST ALWAYS ACTUALLY WANTS: the one column
  holding a value that needs more than `===` gets the comparison it needs without every other
  column of every row paying for it. Sixteen columns, one of which needs an equality that is
  not `===`, written both ways. ONE OPERATION IS ONE UNCHANGED `updateEntity` OF SIXTEEN
  COLUMNS. Ours: 16 calls to the row comparator, of which 15 return on its `a === b` first
  line and one runs the expensive body. Floor: 16 `Map.prototype.get` probes, 15 inline `===`
  comparisons, and one call into the same expensive body. THE DIFFERENCE IS FIFTEEN CALLS
  AGAINST SIXTEEN MAP PROBES; the expensive comparison is charged once to both arms and
  cancels. THE EXPENSIVE COMPARATOR IS A CASE-INSENSITIVE STRING COMPARE AND NOT A DEEP EQUAL,
  on purpose: a deeper comparator would raise both nanosecond columns by the same amount and
  pull the ratio toward 1.00, so this row is the map design's best case. THE WRITE AROUND THE
  WALK IS IDENTICAL ON BOTH ARMS AND IS INSIDE BOTH NUMBERS: `assertWritable` with the
  channel's `assertNotFlushing`, `openSubframe`/`closeSubframe`, the writer dispatch, the
  `length > 0` test, and — on a write that moves something — the changed-names array,
  `markColumnsChanged`'s column-store probe per moved column, `channel.writeWithoutFlushCheck`
  and `markContentChanged`. That is a large constant added to the numerator and the
  denominator alike, so it pulls this ratio toward 1.00 and the printed ratio is a LOWER BOUND
  on the difference between the two walks. The two nanosecond columns beside it are not pulled
  anywhere: subtract them to get the per-write difference the row is actually about. THIS PAIR
  DECLARES `isolate`, AND THE READING IS UNINTERPRETABLE WITHOUT IT. Both arms reach the
  engine through one compiled function — `OIMEntityCollection.prototype.patchEntity`, whose
  single call site `this.entityWriter.patchEntity(entitySlot, patch)` would see both writers
  in one process. Neither would then be inlined, and inlining the column walk into the write
  is exactly where a walk's advantage lives; worse, the failure is invisible, because a ratio
  of 1.00 would be equally consistent with "the two walks cost the same" and "each arm ran
  through caches the other had already made polymorphic". Each side is therefore probed and
  timed in a process that has never constructed the other, and `build` declines to fill the
  population it was not asked for — merely FILLING the other design's population teaches V8
  its shape, so declining to time it is not enough. What it costs: `noise` now includes
  process-to-process variation, which is the honest thing for it to include here.
  measured  324,536 ops/pass ours, 234,570 floor; 9 samples; warm-up 20 planned, 20/20 reached; spread 3.5%/2.6%; load 2.62 → 2.62 on 8 cores

alt.slot-literal.complete-vs-grown.mint-a-population   [per relationship]   0.91x
  verdict  the shipped design wins — 1.10x cheaper
  ours     upsertEntity of a key that has none — the shipped fifteen-field entity slot literal
  floor    REJECTED: a slot minted with its ten valued fields, the rest added on first use
  THE FIRST OF THE TWO READINGS THIS DECISION ASKS FOR, AND IT IS THE ONE THAT SHOULD GO
  AGAINST US. `createOIMEntitySlot` returns fifteen fields, five of them born`undefined` —
  `effects`, `entity`, `columnSlotStore`, `data`, `indexKeys` — and the rejected design (a
  subclass overriding `createSlot`) leaves all five out and lets the engine add each on first
  assignment. So ours pays five stores per identity the floor does not, and if this row does
  not come back ABOVE 1.00 the two designs do not differ where the argument says they do. ONE
  OPERATION IS ONE `upsertEntity` OF A KEY THAT HAS NO VALUE — the whole first-load path:
  `writeContentByKey` minting the identity, the copying writer strategy, the store, the marks.
  The pass CONSUMES its fixture, so both sides declare `operationsPerPass` and rebuild an
  empty population in the untimed `reset`. ONE DIFFERENCE THAT IS NOT THE LITERAL, in the
  FLOOR'S disfavour: on the floor `slot.entity =` ADDS a property rather than storing into
  one, a map transition per row — which is what "added on first use" means. WHAT THIS ROW
  CANNOT SAY is what the unused fields COST in bytes; that is `npm run retained`, and this
  pair does not stand in for it. THIS PAIR DECLARES `isolate`, AND THE READING IS
  UNINTERPRETABLE WITHOUT IT. Both arms reach the engine through one compiled function —
  `OIMEntityCollection.prototype.patchEntity`, whose single call site
  `this.entityWriter.patchEntity(entitySlot, patch)` would see both writers in one process.
  Neither would then be inlined, and inlining the column walk into the write is exactly where
  a walk's advantage lives; worse, the failure is invisible, because a ratio of 1.00 would be
  equally consistent with "the two walks cost the same" and "each arm ran through caches the
  other had already made polymorphic". Each side is therefore probed and timed in a process
  that has never constructed the other, and `build` declines to fill the population it was not
  asked for — merely FILLING the other design's population teaches V8 its shape, so declining
  to time it is not enough. What it costs: `noise` now includes process-to-process variation,
  which is the honest thing for it to include here.
  measured  8,192 ops/pass ours, 8,192 floor; 15 samples; warm-up 616 planned, 616/616 reached; spread 49.6%/62.7%; load 2.45 → 2.66 on 8 cores

alt.bucket-coalescing.one-bucket-64-adds-with-a-listener   [per row per write]   0.92x
  verdict  the shipped design wins — 1.08x cheaper
  ours     64 adds to one bucket in one subframe, one content-change listener, coalesced: the bucket marked pending, flushed by a subframe-close callback
  floor    the same adds, each one written to the channel, settled and announced at once
  64 adds to ONE bucket inside one subframe, one content-change listener on the bucket owner.
  Without coalescing each add is still buffered by the owner's own subframe and delivered at
  the close as onContentChangedMany with one entry per add, so the listener hears 64 changes
  of one bucket. The shipped arm marks the bucket pending once, registers a close callback in
  the channel's Set on every add, and writes, settles and announces the bucket once when the
  outermost subframe closes; the alternative does all three on every add. One operation is one
  member add. Both arms open the same nested subframe around each add, push the member into
  the bucket store, and close the frame after every outer subframe; emptying the stores there
  is timed on both arms alike. The bucket slot is held and has one effect, so the channel
  write door takes its effect path: the first write of a frame schedules the effect and the
  rest find it scheduled. The bodies copy OIMBucketWriter.markBucketSlotChanged,
  OIMChannel.writeWithoutFlushCheck and closeSubframe, and OIMSlotContentOwner.settleWrite,
  contentChanged and its subframe delivery, as of 2026-10-21. The release policy is the
  default one, which does nothing on a write, so neither arm calls it.

alt.replacing-build.decide-first-vs-build-as-you-walk.every-column-moved   [per write]   0.99x
  verdict  UNSETTLED — the difference is inside this run's own error. Neither design is shown to win.
  ours     the replacing writer: walk to decide, then one spread and one store per moved column
  floor    REJECTED: one spread of the entity up front, then assign each column as it is found
  WHAT THE SHIPPED ORDER COSTS WHEN THE WRITE REALLY CHANGES SOMETHING — a re-hydration, where
  every write changes something. ONE OPERATION IS ONE `updateEntity` MOVING ALL SIXTEEN
  COLUMNS. Both arms: 16 `oimIsProtoKey` calls, 16 equality calls, one changed-names array
  with 16 pushes, the marks, and exactly ONE record allocated with 17 values spread and 16
  stored. The difference is that ours walks the patch to decide and then loops the 16 recorded
  names a second time to store them, where the floor stores as it walks. IF IT COMES BACK NEAR
  1.00 the second loop is worth nothing and the rejected design loses on its own ground as
  well as on the unchanged-write contract. THE WRITE AROUND THE WALK IS IDENTICAL ON BOTH ARMS
  AND IS INSIDE BOTH NUMBERS: `assertWritable` with the channel's `assertNotFlushing`,
  `openSubframe`/`closeSubframe`, the writer dispatch, the `length > 0` test, and — on a write
  that moves something — the changed-names array, `markColumnsChanged`'s column-store probe
  per moved column, `channel.writeWithoutFlushCheck` and `markContentChanged`. That is a large
  constant added to the numerator and the denominator alike, so it pulls this ratio toward
  1.00 and the printed ratio is a LOWER BOUND on the difference between the two walks. The two
  nanosecond columns beside it are not pulled anywhere: subtract them to get the per-write
  difference the row is actually about. THIS PAIR DECLARES `isolate`, AND THE READING IS
  UNINTERPRETABLE WITHOUT IT. Both arms reach the engine through one compiled function —
  `OIMEntityCollection.prototype.patchEntity`, whose single call site
  `this.entityWriter.patchEntity(entitySlot, patch)` would see both writers in one process.
  Neither would then be inlined, and inlining the column walk into the write is exactly where
  a walk's advantage lives; worse, the failure is invisible, because a ratio of 1.00 would be
  equally consistent with "the two walks cost the same" and "each arm ran through caches the
  other had already made polymorphic". Each side is therefore probed and timed in a process
  that has never constructed the other, and `build` declines to fill the population it was not
  asked for — merely FILLING the other design's population teaches V8 its shape, so declining
  to time it is not enough. What it costs: `noise` now includes process-to-process variation,
  which is the honest thing for it to include here.
  measured  65,536 ops/pass ours, 65,536 floor; 9 samples; warm-up 28 planned, 28/28 reached; spread 4.7%/89.6%; load 2.50 → 2.73 on 8 cores

alt.slot-literal.complete-vs-grown.write-a-partly-subscribed-population   [per write]   1.03x
  verdict  UNSETTLED — the difference is inside this run's own error. Neither design is shown to win.
  ours     updateEntity on a loaded population whose every slot has one shape
  floor    REJECTED: the same write where a slot’s shape depends on what has happened to it
  WHERE THE SHIPPED LITERAL IS SUPPOSED TO EARN THE FIVE STORES THE ROW ABOVE CHARGES IT FOR.
  "Every slot of a population settles on a single hidden class" is only worth anything if
  something later reads those slots through a site that would otherwise go polymorphic — which
  is the write path: `channel.writeWithoutFlushCheck` reads `effects`, `changedAtRevision` and
  `changedAtEpoch`, and the collection reads `entity` and `isReleased`, all through one
  compiled body per method. THE POPULATION IS DELIBERATELY HETEROGENEOUS THE WAY A SCREEN
  MAKES IT SO: one row in eight carries an effect (`effectService.createEffect([slot], [],
  body)`), which is what a mounted view of a list looks like. On ours that changes no shape —
  `effects` was there from birth. On the floor the subscribed slots gain a property the rest
  do not have, so the population holds two shapes and every one of those reads sees both. ONE
  OPERATION IS ONE `updateEntity` MOVING ONE COLUMN of a sixteen-column row, and the
  subscribed eighth really is woken — both arms subscribe the same rows to the same shared
  body, so the delivery is inside both numbers and cancels. THE FLOOR IS AT ITS BEST WITH TWO
  SHAPES; a column hold (`columnSlotStore`) or an index (`indexKeys`) adds another at the same
  sites, so this reading is a LOWER BOUND on what the shipped literal buys. THIS PAIR DECLARES
  `isolate`, AND THE READING IS UNINTERPRETABLE WITHOUT IT. Both arms reach the engine through
  one compiled function — `OIMEntityCollection.prototype.patchEntity`, whose single call site
  `this.entityWriter.patchEntity(entitySlot, patch)` would see both writers in one process.
  Neither would then be inlined, and inlining the column walk into the write is exactly where
  a walk's advantage lives; worse, the failure is invisible, because a ratio of 1.00 would be
  equally consistent with "the two walks cost the same" and "each arm ran through caches the
  other had already made polymorphic". Each side is therefore probed and timed in a process
  that has never constructed the other, and `build` declines to fill the population it was not
  asked for — merely FILLING the other design's population teaches V8 its shape, so declining
  to time it is not enough. What it costs: `noise` now includes process-to-process variation,
  which is the honest thing for it to include here.
  measured  794,874 ops/pass ours, 908,750 floor; 9 samples; warm-up 34 planned, 34/34 reached; spread 61.4%/53.3%; load 2.66 → 2.68 on 8 cores

  verdict  !  THE REJECTED DESIGN WINS — 1.13x DEARER. This decision came out wrong.
  ours     8 adds to one bucket in one subframe, one content-change listener, coalesced: the bucket marked pending, flushed by a subframe-close callback
  floor    adaptive by listener: with no content-change listener every change is written, settled and announced at once, and the bucket is pushed once into the channel-owned array only when it is releasable (unheld); with a listener it behaves as the adaptive arm
  8 adds to ONE bucket inside one subframe, one content-change listener on the bucket owner.

  one bucket. Held scenarios never reach the array on this arm when no listener exists, so
  they price the immediate path plus one listener-count check and one hold-count check per
  add. Unheld scenarios also pay one push per bucket and its release check at the close. The
  model reads releasability as holdCount 0; the shipped release policy is the default, which
  releases an unheld bucket only when it is empty. One operation is one member add. Both arms
  open the same nested subframe around each add, push the member into the bucket store, and
  close the frame after every outer subframe; emptying the stores there is timed on both arms
  alike. The bucket slot is held and has one effect, so the channel write door takes its
  effect path: the first write of a frame schedules the effect and the rest find it scheduled.

  closeSubframe, and OIMSlotContentOwner.settleWrite, contentChanged and its subframe

  verdict  !  THE REJECTED DESIGN WINS — 1.16x DEARER. This decision came out wrong.

  floor    the same walk of the same members, kept as a list, per member
  THE SENTENCE UNDER TEST is the one printed above the published removal table: "Both iterate
  at around [NO RUN BEHIND THIS FIGURE] 2 ns per element, and the difference between them is a
  fraction of that." It is the half of the bucket service argument that says the choice is NOT
  an iteration question, and it had no measurement behind it either. THE BODY IS THE SAME ON
  BOTH HALVES, character for character: one `getBucketMemberSlots`, one for-of, one field load
  and one store into the sink per member. The only difference in the process is what
  `getBucketMemberSlots` hands back — a `Set` on ours, the service's own array on the floor —
  so this row is a property of two JavaScript containers reached through one library, which is
  exactly what the sentence claims to be about. NOTHING IS WRITTEN, NO MEMBERSHIP IS SET AND
  NO FRAME IS CLOSED, and neither service orders anything on a read: the list hands back the
  array `priceGroupIndex` filled, in the order it was written. What a pass pays outside the
  walk is one service call and one iterator, spread over 10000 members. DIRECTION, three ways:
  (1) ours is measured on a set that was FILLED and never deleted from, so its entry table has
  no tombstones to walk — a bucket that has churned iterates worse, and this row therefore
  flatters the unordered half; (2) `slot.entity` is loaded per member on both halves, because
  a reader that visits members without looking at them is not a reader, and it is the same
  monomorphic field load on each side; (3) a pass is a whole walk of the bucket, so the
  operation count may overshoot what the prober asked for by fewer than 10000 members out of
  the millions a [NO RUN BEHIND THIS FIGURE] 25 ms pass visits.

alt.settle.pull-at-the-getter-vs-push-at-the-write.depth-1   [per frame]   1.19x
  verdict  !  THE REJECTED DESIGN WINS — 1.19x DEARER. This decision came out wrong.
  ours     write 300 roots, then read 200 computeds that walk their 3 sources — chain depth 1
  floor    the same writes, each marking its dependents dirty; each read is one boolean — depth 1
  THE ROW THE RECOVERED RUN ACTUALLY COVERED, REBUILT SO THE OTHER TWO HAVE SOMETHING TO BE
  READ AGAINST. Its figure at this geometry was 0.72x in our favour [NO RUN BEHIND THIS
  FIGURE], and the whole reason the other two rows exist is that this one was the ONLY depth
  measured. At depth 1 push is at its strongest: a consumer is one boolean away from knowing
  it owes a derivation, and the sources under it are roots, which are current by definition —
  so the arm has nothing to descend and nothing to bring up to date. If the recovered figure
  is going to reproduce anywhere it is here, and a reading far from it on this row means the
  two runs are not measuring the same thing and the other two rows should not be quoted either
  [FIGURE STATED IN ADVANCE]. RESIDUALS. AGAINST US, AND THEY ARE THE MAJORITY: our arm is the
  SHIPPED `OIMComputedCollection`, one per level, each node a held computed slot read through
  `getValueForSlot`, so every read pays `assertNotReleased` and a call into
  `oimRefreshComputedSlot`; every refresh that misses the clock pays
  `oimRefreshSourceComputeds`, a call into `oimHasSourceChangedSinceLastRun`, a call into
  `oimIsRevisionAfter` per source, a call into the comparator, `isDeriving` written twice and
  THREE pairs of stamps written per derivation (`ranAt*`, `checkedAt*` and `changedAt*`) — the
  rejected arm carries one boolean and writes it once. Above level one the derive reads each
  source computed through its owner AGAIN, after `oimRefreshSourceComputeds` already brought
  it current, so each such source costs one more `getValueForSlot` that returns on its
  `checkedAt` pair, and the derive finds its source list by the slot's key. Our nodes are also
  filed as slots in a `Map` per level, with `effects`, `holdCount`, `owner` and `key` on every
  one of them, and the rejected node has none of that. AGAINST THE REJECTED ARM: nothing,
  except that it walks a recursive `markDependents` where a real engine might flatten it. WHAT
  NEITHER ARM PAYS: the frame. No effect is subscribed and `channel.flush()` is never called,
  so the leaf list, the drain and the after-frame phases are on neither side — a frame is
  priced by `flush.empty-vs-noop` and adding it to both halves of a ratio only pulls it toward
  1. WHAT IS NOT EXERCISED, and it is the design difference that no timing here can show: push
  marks the WHOLE transitive cone at the write, while pull stops at the first computed whose
  value held. Every root moves every frame here and every sum therefore changes, so the
  comparator never cuts anything on either arm and push is never charged for a mark it did not
  owe. A graph where a change is absorbed part-way up is the arrangement in which push is at
  its worst, and it is not measured. NOR IS THE CONSUMER AXIS: the recovered table reported
  the two converging where ten consumers share one computed, and every node here is read by
  exactly one consumer.
  measured  7,212 ops/pass ours, 7,110 floor; 9 samples; warm-up 24 planned, 24/24 reached; spread 0.5%/59.3%; load 2.00 → 2.00 on 8 cores

  64 adds to ONE bucket inside one subframe, one content-change listener on the bucket owner.
  Without coalescing each add is still buffered by the owner's own subframe and delivered at
  the close as onContentChangedMany with one entry per add, so the listener hears 64 changes
  of one bucket. Held scenarios never reach the array on this arm when no listener exists, so
  they price the immediate path plus one listener-count check and one hold-count check per
  add. Unheld scenarios also pay one push per bucket and its release check at the close. The
  model reads releasability as holdCount 0; the shipped release policy is the default, which
  releases an unheld bucket only when it is empty. One operation is one member add. Both arms
  open the same nested subframe around each add, push the member into the bucket store, and
  close the frame after every outer subframe; emptying the stores there is timed on both arms
  alike. The bucket slot is held and has one effect, so the channel write door takes its
  effect path: the first write of a frame schedules the effect and the rest find it scheduled.
  The bodies copy OIMBucketWriter.markBucketSlotChanged, OIMChannel.writeWithoutFlushCheck and
  closeSubframe, and OIMSlotContentOwner.settleWrite, contentChanged and its subframe
  delivery, as of 2026-10-21. The release policy is the default one, which does nothing on a
  write, so neither arm calls it.
  measured  2,050,136 ops/pass ours, 2,097,152 floor; 9 samples; warm-up 22 planned, 22/22 reached; spread 1.1%/2.3%; load 2.53 → 2.53 on 8 cores

alt.bucket-coalescing.adaptive.one-bucket-64-adds   [per row per write]   1.20x
  verdict  !  THE REJECTED DESIGN WINS — 1.20x DEARER. This decision came out wrong.
  ours     64 adds to one bucket in one subframe, coalesced: the bucket marked pending, flushed by a subframe-close callback
  floor    adaptive coalescing: a bucket's first change in a batch is written, settled and announced at once and the bucket is pushed once into a channel-owned array; later changes in the same subframe only flag it, and the outermost close writes and announces each flagged bucket once more, then runs its release check
  64 adds to ONE bucket inside one subframe. No content-change listener. Every scenario here
  opens an outer subframe, so the adaptive arm always takes its batch path: the first change
  of each bucket costs what the immediate arm pays, and from the second change on it pays one
  flag write per add plus one more write and announce per bucket at the close. With a
  listener, the first announce is buffered by the owner's subframe and the second reaches the
  listener directly at the channel close, so a bucket changed more than once is heard twice. A
  single-item door with no outer subframe takes the immediate path and is not among these
  scenarios. One operation is one member add. Both arms open the same nested subframe around
  each add, push the member into the bucket store, and close the frame after every outer

  schedules the effect and the rest find it scheduled. The bodies copy
  OIMBucketWriter.markBucketSlotChanged, OIMChannel.writeWithoutFlushCheck and closeSubframe,

  Both sides run on the same population of priceDataset.batch entities and visit the same ids
  in the same order. The floor reaches the same slots through a hand-built Map<id, slot>; the

alt.equality-predicate.shared-default-vs-inlined.with-a-second-comparator-present   [per write]   1.42x
  verdict  !  THE REJECTED DESIGN WINS — 1.42x DEARER. This decision came out wrong.
  ours     the same write, in a process where one other population has its own comparator
  floor    REJECTED: SameValueZero written into the walk — nothing can spoil it
  THE HALF OF THE ALTERNATIVE THE ROW ABOVE CANNOT SEE. `createOIMEntityWriterInPlace` returns
  a closure over ONE compiled `patchEntityInPlace` and ONE `changedColumns`, so the
  `sameValueGuard(entity[name], source[name])` inside it is one call site for the whole
  process — and one population built with its own comparator makes it polymorphic for every
  population that did not. A design with no parameter cannot be spoiled that way. HOW THE
  SPOILER IS APPLIED, outside the clock: a second population of 64 rows is built with
  `createOIMEntityWriterInPlace({ sameValueGuard: caseInsensitiveEquality })` and written to
  in this side's `reset`, which is never timed. The timed body touches only the measured
  population, so the WORK on the two arms is identical and the difference from the row above
  is the state of one inline cache. THE FLOOR IS THE SAME FLOOR AS THE ROW ABOVE,
  deliberately. A CAVEAT AGAINST THIS ROW: nothing here forces V8 to keep the site
  polymorphic; if the two rows come back identical within the noise floor, that is the first
  thing to suspect and the second is that polymorphism at this site is genuinely free. THE
  WRITE AROUND THE WALK IS IDENTICAL ON BOTH ARMS AND IS INSIDE BOTH NUMBERS: `assertWritable`
  with the channel's `assertNotFlushing`, `openSubframe`/`closeSubframe`, the writer dispatch,
  the `length > 0` test, and — on a write that moves something — the changed-names array,
  `markColumnsChanged`'s column-store probe per moved column, `channel.writeWithoutFlushCheck`
  and `markContentChanged`. That is a large constant added to the numerator and the
  denominator alike, so it pulls this ratio toward 1.00 and the printed ratio is a LOWER BOUND
  on the difference between the two walks. The two nanosecond columns beside it are not pulled
  anywhere: subtract them to get the per-write difference the row is actually about. THIS PAIR
  DECLARES `isolate`, AND THE READING IS UNINTERPRETABLE WITHOUT IT. Both arms reach the
  engine through one compiled function — `OIMEntityCollection.prototype.patchEntity`, whose
  single call site `this.entityWriter.patchEntity(entitySlot, patch)` would see both writers
  in one process. Neither would then be inlined, and inlining the column walk into the write
  is exactly where a walk's advantage lives; worse, the failure is invisible, because a ratio
  of 1.00 would be equally consistent with "the two walks cost the same" and "each arm ran
  through caches the other had already made polymorphic". Each side is therefore probed and
  timed in a process that has never constructed the other, and `build` declines to fill the
  population it was not asked for — merely FILLING the other design's population teaches V8
  its shape, so declining to time it is not enough. What it costs: `noise` now includes
  process-to-process variation, which is the honest thing for it to include here.
  measured  209,152 ops/pass ours, 340,262 floor; 9 samples; warm-up 25 planned, 25/25 reached; spread 5.7%/7.6%; load 2.68 → 2.68 on 8 cores

alt.settle.pull-at-the-getter-vs-push-at-the-write.depth-2   [per frame]   1.52x
  verdict  !  THE REJECTED DESIGN WINS — 1.52x DEARER. This decision came out wrong.
  ours     the same frame over a computed reading a computed — chain depth 2
  floor    the same frame, marked at the write and descended by the flag — chain depth 2
  THE FIRST DEPTH THE RECOVERED RUN NEVER REACHED, AND THE ONE WHERE PUSH STARTS PAYING TWICE.
  A write now marks 200 nodes at level one and 200 more above them, and the walk is recursive;
  our arm's write is unchanged — it stamps a root and stops — while its READ grows by one
  level. So the two curves cross somewhere if they cross at all, and this row and the next are
  what say whether they do. WHAT DOES NOT CHANGE BETWEEN THE ROWS: the population, the fan-in,
  the number of consumers, the writes per frame and the derive body. Only the number of levels
  between the roots and the consumers moves, so this row minus the depth-1 row is one level,
  on both arms, in one process. IF THE RATIO RISES TOWARD 1.00 AS DEPTH GROWS THE VERDICT IS
  NARROWER THAN THE RECOVERED TABLE CLAIMED [FIGURE STATED IN ADVANCE], and if it crosses 1.00
  the decision is wrong for deep chains and that is the finding this file was built to be able
  to report. RESIDUALS. AGAINST US, AND THEY ARE THE MAJORITY: our arm is the SHIPPED
  `OIMComputedCollection`, one per level, each node a held computed slot read through
  `getValueForSlot`, so every read pays `assertNotReleased` and a call into
  `oimRefreshComputedSlot`; every refresh that misses the clock pays
  `oimRefreshSourceComputeds`, a call into `oimHasSourceChangedSinceLastRun`, a call into
  `oimIsRevisionAfter` per source, a call into the comparator, `isDeriving` written twice and
  THREE pairs of stamps written per derivation (`ranAt*`, `checkedAt*` and `changedAt*`) — the
  rejected arm carries one boolean and writes it once. Above level one the derive reads each
  source computed through its owner AGAIN, after `oimRefreshSourceComputeds` already brought
  it current, so each such source costs one more `getValueForSlot` that returns on its
  `checkedAt` pair, and the derive finds its source list by the slot's key. Our nodes are also
  filed as slots in a `Map` per level, with `effects`, `holdCount`, `owner` and `key` on every
  one of them, and the rejected node has none of that. AGAINST THE REJECTED ARM: nothing,
  except that it walks a recursive `markDependents` where a real engine might flatten it. WHAT
  NEITHER ARM PAYS: the frame. No effect is subscribed and `channel.flush()` is never called,
  so the leaf list, the drain and the after-frame phases are on neither side — a frame is
  priced by `flush.empty-vs-noop` and adding it to both halves of a ratio only pulls it toward
  1. WHAT IS NOT EXERCISED, and it is the design difference that no timing here can show: push
  marks the WHOLE transitive cone at the write, while pull stops at the first computed whose
  value held. Every root moves every frame here and every sum therefore changes, so the
  comparator never cuts anything on either arm and push is never charged for a mark it did not
  owe. A graph where a change is absorbed part-way up is the arrangement in which push is at
  its worst, and it is not measured. NOR IS THE CONSUMER AXIS: the recovered table reported
  the two converging where ten consumers share one computed, and every node here is read by
  exactly one consumer.
  measured  2,048 ops/pass ours, 2,048 floor; 9 samples; warm-up 29 planned, 29/29 reached; spread 0.8%/3.3%; load 1.92 → 2.25 on 8 cores

alt.coalesce-vs-eager.1-writes-per-node-per-frame   [per frame]   1.60x
  verdict  !  THE REJECTED DESIGN WINS — 1.60x DEARER. This decision came out wrong.
  ours     mark the node at the write, recompute each marked node once when the frame closes
  floor    recompute the node at every write
  ONE OPERATION IS ONE FRAME over the whole node population, each node taking the stated
  number of writes to its sources. Coalescing pays a flag test and a push per write and one
  recompute per node per frame; eager pays one recompute per write and nothing else. So the
  answer is a BREAK-EVEN in writes per node per frame, not a speed-up: at one write eager does
  strictly less, and every row above it is where coalescing has to earn its list. Both arms
  are plain objects; both were checked to leave every node holding the same value after a
  frame.

alt.settle.pull-at-the-getter-vs-push-at-the-write.depth-3   [per frame]   1.63x
  verdict  !  THE REJECTED DESIGN WINS — 1.63x DEARER. This decision came out wrong.
  ours     the same frame over a computed reading a computed reading a computed — chain depth 3
  floor    the same frame, marked at the write through three levels — chain depth 3
  THE DEPTH THE RECOVERY ENTRY NAMES BY HAND — "a computed over a computed over a computed was
  not measured" — and the far end of the only axis that could reverse the decision. 600 nodes
  now stand between 300 roots and 200 consumers. THE ARITHMETIC THE ROW IS CHECKING: push pays
  its marking over the whole cone at every frame's first write and then reads one boolean per
  node; we pay nothing at the write and one comparison per source at the read. Those are the
  same order of magnitude, so which wins is a question about constants and not about shapes,
  which is exactly the kind of question a measurement settles and an operation count does not.
  THIS IS THE ROW TO DISTRUST IF ANY: three levels of 200 nodes is deeper than most screens
  go, and a reader taking a verdict from here alone has chosen the extreme of the axis. Read
  the three rows as a table. RESIDUALS. AGAINST US, AND THEY ARE THE MAJORITY: our arm is the
  SHIPPED `OIMComputedCollection`, one per level, each node a held computed slot read through
  `getValueForSlot`, so every read pays `assertNotReleased` and a call into
  `oimRefreshComputedSlot`; every refresh that misses the clock pays
  `oimRefreshSourceComputeds`, a call into `oimHasSourceChangedSinceLastRun`, a call into
  `oimIsRevisionAfter` per source, a call into the comparator, `isDeriving` written twice and
  THREE pairs of stamps written per derivation (`ranAt*`, `checkedAt*` and `changedAt*`) — the
  rejected arm carries one boolean and writes it once. Above level one the derive reads each
  source computed through its owner AGAIN, after `oimRefreshSourceComputeds` already brought
  it current, so each such source costs one more `getValueForSlot` that returns on its
  `checkedAt` pair, and the derive finds its source list by the slot's key. Our nodes are also
  filed as slots in a `Map` per level, with `effects`, `holdCount`, `owner` and `key` on every
  one of them, and the rejected node has none of that. AGAINST THE REJECTED ARM: nothing,
  except that it walks a recursive `markDependents` where a real engine might flatten it. WHAT
  NEITHER ARM PAYS: the frame. No effect is subscribed and `channel.flush()` is never called,
  so the leaf list, the drain and the after-frame phases are on neither side — a frame is
  priced by `flush.empty-vs-noop` and adding it to both halves of a ratio only pulls it toward
  1. WHAT IS NOT EXERCISED, and it is the design difference that no timing here can show: push
  marks the WHOLE transitive cone at the write, while pull stops at the first computed whose
  value held. Every root moves every frame here and every sum therefore changes, so the
  comparator never cuts anything on either arm and push is never charged for a mark it did not
  owe. A graph where a change is absorbed part-way up is the arrangement in which push is at
  its worst, and it is not measured. NOR IS THE CONSUMER AXIS: the recovered table reported
  the two converging where ten consumers share one computed, and every node here is read by
  exactly one consumer.
  measured  1,024 ops/pass ours, 2,048 floor; 9 samples; warm-up 29 planned, 29/29 reached; spread 1.1%/0.8%; load 2.25 → 2.25 on 8 cores

alt.bucket-coalescing.one-bucket-1-adds-with-a-listener   [per row per write]   1.69x
  verdict  !  THE REJECTED DESIGN WINS — 1.69x DEARER. This decision came out wrong.
  ours     1 adds to one bucket in one subframe, one content-change listener, coalesced: the bucket marked pending, flushed by a subframe-close callback
  floor    the same adds, each one written to the channel, settled and announced at once
  1 adds to ONE bucket inside one subframe, one content-change listener on the bucket owner.
  Without coalescing each add is still buffered by the owner's own subframe and delivered at
  the close as onContentChangedMany with one entry per add, so the listener hears 1 changes of
  one bucket. The shipped arm marks the bucket pending once, registers a close callback in the
  channel's Set on every add, and writes, settles and announces the bucket once when the
  outermost subframe closes; the alternative does all three on every add. One operation is one
  member add. Both arms open the same nested subframe around each add, push the member into
  the bucket store, and close the frame after every outer subframe; emptying the stores there
  is timed on both arms alike. The bucket slot is held and has one effect, so the channel
  write door takes its effect path: the first write of a frame schedules the effect and the
  rest find it scheduled. The bodies copy OIMBucketWriter.markBucketSlotChanged,
  OIMChannel.writeWithoutFlushCheck and closeSubframe, and OIMSlotContentOwner.settleWrite,
  contentChanged and its subframe delivery, as of 2026-10-21. The release policy is the
  default one, which does nothing on a write, so neither arm calls it.

  ours     a delivery list built at the write — the frame walks only what was marked
  floor    a stamp written at the write — the frame scans every subscriber for this frame's stamp
  ONE OPERATION IS ONE FRAME: the stated number of subscribers out of the population are
  marked, spread evenly, and the frame delivers to exactly those. The list pays a flag test
  and a push per mark and walks only the marked; the scan pays one store per mark and walks

alt.remove-member.unordered-vs-ordered.50k   [per row per write]   2.37x
  verdict  !  THE REJECTED DESIGN WINS — 2.37x DEARER. This decision came out wrong.
  ours     a member leaves a bucket of 50000 and joins another, membership in a Set (OIMBucketSlotsServiceSetDriven)
  floor    the same move, membership in a list kept in the caller's order (OIMBucketSlotsServiceArrayDriven)
  A BUCKET OF 50000, and the size is in the id because the answer is a function of it on BOTH
  halves. THE COUNT THIS PREDICTS, per member moved, through
  `bucketCollection.bucketContentService.writeBucketMemberSlots`: on the ordered half,
  `reconcileSlots` compares the held list against the one handed in up to the swapped band,
  finds them unequal, drains all 50000 members and pushes all 50000 back, and the collection
  holds 50000 arrivals and unholds 50000 departures. On the unordered half: `reconcileSlots`
  builds one Set of the 50000 slots handed in, probes it once per held member, deletes the one
  departure and adds the one arrival, and one hold is taken and one given back. The ordered
  half's sequence comparison stops at the swapped band, which is why the sweep over 16
  positions (0, 3125, 6250, … 46875) exists: it averages that comparison length. ONE FLUSH IS
  SPREAD OVER 2 OPERATIONS, since a frame swaps one band in every lane at once;
  `flush.empty-vs-noop` prices a flush directly, for a reader who wants it out of the figure.
  WHAT AN OPERATION IS: one member moves from one bucket to another, through the only door the
  engine offers for it — two array stores that exchange the member between the two lists this
  file holds, `bucketCollection.bucketContentService.writeBucketMemberSlots` of each of those
  lists, a flush, and a read of every bucket in the frame through `getBucketMemberSlots`. Both
  halves run the identical body; the only difference in the process is which bucket service
  object the collection was constructed with. READINGS RECORDED BEFORE 2026-08-23 priced a
  re-file reached through a derived membership source and are not this operation. EVERY
  OPERATION REALLY MOVES A MEMBER, which is the failure this suite has already published a
  wrong number for: `writeBucketMemberSlots` RETURNS without a wake when `reconcileSlots`
  finds nothing arrived and nothing departed, and both halves would then time a frame in which
  no container was touched. Here the two lists handed in differ from what the buckets hold by
  exactly one member each, and `assertBandsAreWhereTheyAreExpected` checks each bucket holds
  exactly the members this file holds for it before the clock starts. The two rows of a swap
  are the two members of one band, one in each page of the lane, and the swap exchanges them —
  which is why this needs no `reset` at all: sizes, positions and the sweep are exactly what
  they were when the pass started, so an empty reset here is a steady state rather than a
  forgotten one. DIRECTION, three ways. (1) The two array stores, the mark, the flush and the
  read call are identical in SHAPE on both halves and inside both numbers, which pulls the
  RATIO toward 1.00, so the two printed nanosecond figures are the numbers to read. (2) ONLY
  THE SET HALF DIFFS: `OIMBucketSlotsServiceSetDriven.reconcileSlots` takes the set difference
  (one Set of the list handed in, one probe per held member) and moves one hold each way,
  while the ordered half's `reconcileSlots` compares the two sequences up to the first
  mismatch, then drains the whole list and re-adds every member, so every member it holds is
  unheld and held again. READINGS RECORDED BEFORE 2026-10-17 timed a set half that drained and
  refilled the whole set and an ordered half that kept a rank sorter and took a multiset
  difference, and are not this operation. (3) Both containers are walked every frame and stay
  in cache, which flatters both. A PASS IS WHOLE FRAMES, because half a swap would leave both
  members of a band in one page and every position after it would be a lie; so a pass may
  overshoot the operation count the prober asked for by fewer operations than there are pages,
  out of the tens of thousands it asks for.
  measured  1,024 ops/pass ours, 1,024 floor; 5 samples; warm-up 5 planned, 1/3 reached; spread 2.9%/7.2%; load 2.58 → 2.58 on 8 cores

  refilled the whole set and an ordered half that kept a rank sorter and took a multiset
  difference, and are not this operation. (3) Both containers are walked every frame and stay
  in cache, which flatters both. A PASS IS WHOLE FRAMES, because half a swap would leave both
  members of a band in one page and every position after it would be a lie; so a pass may
  overshoot the operation count the prober asked for by fewer operations than there are pages,
  out of the tens of thousands it asks for.
  measured  1,024 ops/pass ours, 1,024 floor; 9 samples; warm-up 19 planned, 19/19 reached; spread 3.4%/96.9%; load 2.52 → 2.52 on 8 cores

alt.memo.node-vs-recompute.cheap-body.1-reads-per-write   [per read]   3.66x
  verdict  !  THE REJECTED DESIGN WINS — 3.66x DEARER. This decision came out wrong.
  ours     getValueForSlot — a held computed slot of an OIMComputedCollection over the list slot, derived once per write
  floor    body(list.rows) — the same body, called on every read, no node
  THE SHAPE `churnChain` HAS: one field of one row, one read per write. The body is cheaper
  than the node's own bookkeeping, so ours loses by roughly the bookkeeping; no verdict about
  derivation can be taken from this cell alone. BOTH ARMS WRITE THE SAME LIST THROUGH
  `channel.write` before each run of reads, so the stamp is charged equally and only the reads
  differ. Ours asks the node, which compares its clock, walks its one source's stamp and runs
  the body once after each write; the floor calls the body on every read. Nothing is
  subscribed, so no frame runs on either side. The list does not change between writes, so the
  node's guard finds the value equal and keeps it — the cheapest outcome for ours after a
  derive. The node is a computed slot resolved before the clock, and every read goes through
  its owner's `getValueForSlot`, which pays `assertNotReleased` before the clock compare; its
  derive finds the list by the slot's key, one array index the floor's direct `list.rows` does
  not pay.
  measured  2,097,152 ops/pass ours, 11,568,792 floor; 9 samples; warm-up 24 planned, 24/24 reached; spread 0.6%/1.5%; load 1.85 → 2.10 on 8 cores

alt.remove-member.unordered-vs-ordered.10k   [per row per write]   3.84x
  verdict  !  THE REJECTED DESIGN WINS — 3.84x DEARER. This decision came out wrong.
  ours     a member leaves a bucket of 10000 and joins another, membership in a Set (OIMBucketSlotsServiceSetDriven)
  floor    the same move, membership in a list kept in the caller's order (OIMBucketSlotsServiceArrayDriven)
  A BUCKET OF 10000, and the size is in the id because the answer is a function of it on BOTH
  halves. THE COUNT THIS PREDICTS, per member moved, through
  `bucketCollection.bucketContentService.writeBucketMemberSlots`: on the ordered half,
  `reconcileSlots` compares the held list against the one handed in up to the swapped band,
  finds them unequal, drains all 10000 members and pushes all 10000 back, and the collection
  holds 10000 arrivals and unholds 10000 departures. On the unordered half: `reconcileSlots`
  builds one Set of the 10000 slots handed in, probes it once per held member, deletes the one
  departure and adds the one arrival, and one hold is taken and one given back. The ordered
  half's sequence comparison stops at the swapped band, which is why the sweep over 16
  positions (0, 625, 1250, … 9375) exists: it averages that comparison length. ONE FLUSH IS
  SPREAD OVER 8 OPERATIONS, since a frame swaps one band in every lane at once;
  `flush.empty-vs-noop` prices a flush directly, for a reader who wants it out of the figure.
  WHAT AN OPERATION IS: one member moves from one bucket to another, through the only door the
  engine offers for it — two array stores that exchange the member between the two lists this
  file holds, `bucketCollection.bucketContentService.writeBucketMemberSlots` of each of those
  lists, a flush, and a read of every bucket in the frame through `getBucketMemberSlots`. Both
  halves run the identical body; the only difference in the process is which bucket service
  object the collection was constructed with. READINGS RECORDED BEFORE 2026-08-23 priced a
  re-file reached through a derived membership source and are not this operation. EVERY
  OPERATION REALLY MOVES A MEMBER, which is the failure this suite has already published a
  wrong number for: `writeBucketMemberSlots` RETURNS without a wake when `reconcileSlots`
  finds nothing arrived and nothing departed, and both halves would then time a frame in which
  no container was touched. Here the two lists handed in differ from what the buckets hold by
  exactly one member each, and `assertBandsAreWhereTheyAreExpected` checks each bucket holds
  exactly the members this file holds for it before the clock starts. The two rows of a swap
  are the two members of one band, one in each page of the lane, and the swap exchanges them —
  which is why this needs no `reset` at all: sizes, positions and the sweep are exactly what
  they were when the pass started, so an empty reset here is a steady state rather than a
  forgotten one. DIRECTION, three ways. (1) The two array stores, the mark, the flush and the
  read call are identical in SHAPE on both halves and inside both numbers, which pulls the
  RATIO toward 1.00, so the two printed nanosecond figures are the numbers to read. (2) ONLY
  THE SET HALF DIFFS: `OIMBucketSlotsServiceSetDriven.reconcileSlots` takes the set difference
  (one Set of the list handed in, one probe per held member) and moves one hold each way,
  while the ordered half's `reconcileSlots` compares the two sequences up to the first
  mismatch, then drains the whole list and re-adds every member, so every member it holds is
  unheld and held again. READINGS RECORDED BEFORE 2026-10-17 timed a set half that drained and
  refilled the whole set and an ordered half that kept a rank sorter and took a multiset
  difference, and are not this operation. (3) Both containers are walked every frame and stay
  in cache, which flatters both. A PASS IS WHOLE FRAMES, because half a swap would leave both
  members of a band in one page and every position after it would be a lie; so a pass may
  overshoot the operation count the prober asked for by fewer operations than there are pages,
  out of the tens of thousands it asks for.
  measured  1,024 ops/pass ours, 1,024 floor; 5 samples; warm-up 5 planned, 5/5 reached; spread 9.6%/13.1%; load 2.48 → 2.59 on 8 cores

alt.memo.node-vs-recompute.cheap-body.16-reads-per-write   [per read]   6.20x

  floor    body(list.rows) — the same body, called on every read, no node
  THE CHEAP BODY READ SIXTEEN TIMES: what a memo saves is a field load, and what it costs per

alt.position-of-a-member-vs-positions-map   [per read]   16.2x
  verdict  !  THE REJECTED DESIGN WINS — 16.2x DEARER. This decision came out wrong.
  ours     bucketCollection.bucketContentService.getBucketMemberSlotPosition(bucket, member) in a list of 1024 — a scan
  floor    the same question answered by a Map to positions — one hash
  THE ONE ROW IN THIS FILE WHERE THE DESIGN WE REJECTED WINS, and it should: a hash against a
  scan of five hundred on average. It is here because a pair that only shows a rejected design
  losing is an argument and not a measurement. WHAT IT DOES NOT SHOW is the call site:
  `remove` asks for a position and splices with it immediately, and that splice is the same
  memmove for both designs, so the row above is what it costs to have the hash at all. The
  members are cycled through with a mask so the scan length is uniform over the list, and the
  build throws unless both designs place every member at the same position. The reading in
  `packages/bench/measurements/2026-08-20-alternatives/positions` was taken on the removed
  positional index and is not re-attributed here.
  measured  206,145 ops/pass ours, 6,189,850 floor; 9 samples; warm-up 27 planned, 27/27 reached; spread 1.9%/15.3%; load 2.08 → 2.39 on 8 cores

THE CONTROLS, IN FULL

control-resolution-2x   1.94x
  ours   two units of arithmetic per iteration
  floor  one unit of arithmetic per iteration
  NOT A PRICE — an instrument check, and it is printed first because nothing below it means
  anything until it passes. The true ratio is a little UNDER 2: both sides pay the driving
  loop once, so the exact expectation is (2u + L) / (u + L) with L the loop and u one unit,
  which at sixteen dependent steps per unit puts it around 1.9. A reading below 1.5 means this
  process could not resolve a deliberate doubling, and the runner voids the report rather than
  printing prices under it.

control-scale-same-body   1.01x
  ours   one unit of arithmetic per iteration
  floor  the same body again, over its own copy of the same state
  NOT A PRICE — the noise floor. The two sides are identical code over identical,
  separately-allocated state, so the true ratio is 1.00 exactly and every departure from it
  printed here was produced by this machine at this moment. The rule that follows is the one
  the whole table is read under: NO RATIO CLOSER TO 1.00 THAN THIS GAP IS A READING. It also
  bounds the per-row noise figure, which is the same idea measured again inside every pair —
  each pair times its floor twice, once before our side and once after, and prints how far the
  two disagreed.
